| [[Brewing waste heat heats the public building]] | Excess heat from the brew house is ducted to heat the new public building; cold storage adds a heat-pump opportunity. But the transmission distance is limited by temperature: high-grade heat travels, low-grade heat does not. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Challenge the perception of industrial architecture as 'boxing']] | [[Waste heat recovery]], [[Heat recovery radius]] |
| [[Bring the client into the iteration, not the result]] | On a commercial developer scheme, the brief's setbacks, height and podium rules were fed into a generative engine; massing options were filtered by daylight, solar, microclimate and view-factor KPIs. The novelty was inviting the client into the process and surfacing KPIs up front. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[The Henning Larsen Playbook for Biobased Buildings]] | — |
| [[Brownfield rules block on-site recycling]] | Czech environmental code for brownfields is stricter than German or English practice, forcing removal and off-site storage of material he regards as safe to reuse in place — costly, and a running argument with authorities. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Industrial Architecture as Living Infrastructure – Why future design is defined by systems, data, and adaptability, not just form]] | [[Embodied carbon and adaptive reuse of logistics buildings]], [[Material reuse share]] |
| [[Brownfields are per mils of the market]] | Having built over 1,000,000 m² on brownfields, he still calls brownfield reuse a conference fashion: against total built space it is a fraction of a percent. Both brownfield and greenfield are needed — and he says plainly greenfield "is of course not the ideal solution". | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Industrial Architecture as Living Infrastructure – Why future design is defined by systems, data, and adaptability, not just form]] | [[Land take of logistics buildings]] |
| [[Build nothing first]] | Following WBCSD's *Halving Carbon Today*, the hierarchy starts with demand reduction — build nothing, reassess the need, don't over-design — then low-carbon materials, then careful use. Industry wants to skip straight to drop-in low-carbon conventional materials, and that path cannot reach the target. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Net Zero That Holds Up – Whole-Life Carbon, Design Choices, and Delivery]] | [[Embodied carbon and adaptive reuse of logistics buildings]] |
| [[Building green on a landfill that vents methane]] | Fælledby sits on a former landfill on Amager; open fire is prohibited because of methane risk, and a clay cap had to be laid over the whole site. The site was also one of the last big green spaces near central Copenhagen, drawing scientific and public opposition to building at all. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[The Henning Larsen Playbook for Biobased Buildings]] | — |
| [[Building services are a whole-life carbon sleeper]] | In Ramboll's light-industrial/warehouse sample, building services are surprisingly significant on a whole-life basis, because MEP typically has to be replaced once or twice over a building's life. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Net Zero That Holds Up – Whole-Life Carbon, Design Choices, and Delivery]] | [[Embodied carbon and adaptive reuse of logistics buildings]], [[Life-cycle impact by stage]], [[Whole-life carbon]] |
| [[Buildings have non-human users]] | A building sits in nature and is inhabited by animals and plants as well as people, so design should actively support those other users. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Nature positive by design – Creating lasting impact by designing for and with nature]] | — |
| [[Buying a building is buying a pig in a poke]] | The framing device: acquiring a company or a property is the same act — an enormous sum spent on something whose hidden defects you are trying to mitigate or at least reflect in the purchase price. The Czech version of the idiom uses a hare rather than a pig. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Beyond the Concrete – Designing Buildings That Last]] | — |
| [[California wrote the warehouse design code]] | AB 98 (2024) imposes statewide standards on logistics developments: 50 ft buffers on industrial-zoned sites and 100 ft on non-industrial sites within 900 ft of sensitive receptors; a minimum 500 ft setback for truck loading bays; buffers formed of a decorative wall or landscaped berm 10 ft or more high with two rows of drought-tolerant trees in 36-inch boxes at max 40 ft spacing; loading bays oriented away from receptors; PV plus battery storage, 10% of car stalls with EV charging and 50% pre-wired for facilities of 250,000 sq ft or more; and an approved truck routing plan before occupancy. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-external">External</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[National Law Review, 2024]] | [[Visual impact and acceptance by neighbours]], [[Distance to housing]], [[Visual impact on neighbours]] |
| [[Car-free core written into the competition entry]] | At Sugar Valley the team fixed a car-free core and a central green spine as competition-stage commitments, which then bound the mixed-use programme around it. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Nature positive by design – Creating lasting impact by designing for and with nature]] | [[Green-core anchoring of mixed uses]] |
| [[Carbon has a horizontal bias]] | Embodied carbon is driven by horizontal elements, because that's where the mass is. In one Ramboll study structure was ~60% of upfront carbon, and within structure, floors dominated. For logistics — vast ground slabs, vast roofs — the horizontal plane is the whole game. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Net Zero That Holds Up – Whole-Life Carbon, Design Choices, and Delivery]] | [[Embodied carbon and adaptive reuse of logistics buildings]], [[Embodied carbon intensity]] |
| [[Carbon is decided in network design, not in the building]] | The biggest lever is proximity — putting the warehouse as close as possible to demand — but with a real trade-off between many small warehouses and few large ones, which is exactly what the multi-year network model resolves. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Why CEE is the Hottest Location in Europe for supply chain, logistics and industrial Development]] | [[Last-mile delivery emissions and traffic]], [[Delivery catchment]] |
| [[Carbon price is a design input, not an accounting footnote]] | Emission allowance/offset costs attached to bought heat will rise, as will gas and fleet fuel, so operating cost for a building being designed today "will grow significantly." | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Beyond the Concrete – Designing Buildings That Last]] | [[Operational energy and on-site renewables]], [[CapEx–OpEx ratio]] |
| [[Carbon rules are killing basements]] | Strict CO2 regulation in Denmark has made below-ground structures difficult: timber cannot go in the ground because of rot, and excavating and transporting soil is carbon-expensive. Parking is migrating from underground to above-ground structures. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[The Henning Larsen Playbook for Biobased Buildings]] | [[Embodied carbon and adaptive reuse of logistics buildings]] |
| [[Carbon tunnel vision, inverted]] | He accepts the standard critique that a carbon-only focus crowds out water, health and inequality, but argues the opposite in practice: interrogating the carbon of a material forces you to ask where it came from and where the energy came from, which opens the wider conversation. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Net Zero That Holds Up – Whole-Life Carbon, Design Choices, and Delivery]] | — |
| [[Certificates are a language for banks]] | Certification's real function is as a common language linking investors, tenants and banks. Banks tie loan volume to rating level: gold unlocks the full sum, silver a smaller one. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Logistics Development in the concept of certification and LCA]] | — |
| [[Certification as a recycling driver]] | He credits BREEAM specifically with pushing industrial buildings toward the highest possible material recycling rates, and says the advantage of industrial buildings is that they are very easy to recycle. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Industrial Architecture as Living Infrastructure – Why future design is defined by systems, data, and adaptability, not just form]] | [[Embodied carbon and adaptive reuse of logistics buildings]], [[Design for disassembly score]] |
| [[Certification as karma and PR]] | Beyond the financing angle, they characterise a large part of green credentialing as company PR — "check out what we did" — with the client effectively buying the problem in order to get some karma for it. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Build Your Own Playground – Architecture where no one looks for it]] | — |
| [[Certification concept as a red-yellow-green map]] | At concept stage the LEED consultant produces a chart splitting credits three ways: green, essentially certain; red, technically impossible (no public transport); and a middle band that will be fought for as documentation deepens. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Criteria for a Green Building – Beyond energy performance LEED - BREEAM - WELL]] | — |
| [[Certification is a tool, not legislation]] | The closing takeaway: certification systems have limits and are not law; resilience is an additional layer; and the decisive input is the designer's critical thinking. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Logistics Development in the concept of certification and LCA]] | — |
| [[Certification is pulled by clients, not pushed by industry]] | Roughly 40 BREEAM certifications in the portfolio, running from building permit through to occupation. He is explicit that certification is not the industry pushing itself — it is client and market demand. The new BREEAM version ties more tightly to EU taxonomy, requires detailed energy management, and bans fossil fuels at the highest rating level. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[From Model to Reality – Sustainability in Industrial Buildings]] | — |
| [[Certification of reused structural elements is the real blocker]] | Without a material passport you must certify each reclaimed element individually before using it structurally; insurers are the other obstacle. A building passport, held in the model, is the fix. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Challenge the perception of industrial architecture as 'boxing']] | [[Embodied carbon and adaptive reuse of logistics buildings]], [[Material reuse share]] |
| [[Certification treats a building as a puzzle]] | Certification does not pronounce a building good or bad; it splits it into pieces (location, envelope, LCA), scores each, and reassembles a comparable total. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Logistics Development in the concept of certification and LCA]] | — |
| [[Charging, not the vehicle, is the electrification bottleneck]] | The main barrier is charging time and infrastructure scarcity causing courier downtime; the NGSeat pilot built a battery-swapping ecosystem around a universal battery usable across more than twenty vehicle types, swapping in under a minute, aiming at up to 90% CO2 reduction, tested with last-mile food couriers. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Integrated Urban Logistics – From last-mile to mobility hubs]] | [[Last-mile delivery emissions and traffic]], [[Last-mile delivery emissions]] |
| [[Checklists invite point-chasing, weighting resists it]] | LEED and BREEAM function more like checklists, so a designer can hunt the highest-scoring categories; DGNB and SBTool recalculate points through weights so the cheapest route to a score is obscured. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Logistics Development in the concept of certification and LCA]] | — |
| [[Circulation spaces defended with sensor data]] | In the US, public or private funding cannot be sought for undefined space, so a Cincinnati university client attacked the atria, stairs and wide corridors as unfundable. A sensor-and-camera research project recorded how students actually used them, reclassifying them as learning spaces — and during the pandemic it was the only new campus building that still worked. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[The Henning Larsen Playbook for Biobased Buildings]] | [[Social interaction index]] |
| [[Cities beyond the climate battle]] | Widening Guterres' line: cities are per se civilisation's most important transformation tool, not just a climate arena. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[From Plot to Place – Designing Thriving and Resilient Neighborhoods]] | — |
| [[Cities grow while their countries shrink]] | Even in European countries with flat or declining populations, city populations keep rising, pulled by younger working-age domestic and foreign migration. Warsaw is the extreme case. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[The Geography of Growth – Cities, AI, and the future of property]] | — |
| [[Cities outgrow their countries, everywhere]] | Of roughly 1,000 major global cities tracked, around 70% outpaced their national economies in GDP and employment growth in 2025; those cities hold about a third of world population but produce roughly 60% of global GDP, over half of high-income households, and nearly two-thirds of globally ranked universities. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[The Geography of Growth – Cities, AI, and the future of property]] | — |
| [[Citizens are the critical infrastructure]] | Against the framing that resilience is an energy or data problem, she names citizens as Europe's most critical infrastructure, and cities as the asset where GDP actually sits. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[From Plot to Place – Designing Thriving and Resilient Neighborhoods]] | — |
| [[Cleaning the harbour was never about swimming]] | The real payoff was unlocking old industrial harbour fronts for housing, and turning the water itself into Copenhagen's largest urban park. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[From Plot to Place – Designing Thriving and Resilient Neighborhoods]] | — |
| [[Client refused to wait 15 years for green]] | A plant growth analysis defined how far plants could climb in one to five years; the resulting horizontal ribs are planter boxes set at those growth intervals. Species vary by façade orientation. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Big Box, Small Footprint – Lessons from Bestseller's Low-Carbon Logistics Campus]] | [[Biodiversity and ecological connectivity on logistics sites]], [[Green plot ratio]] |
| [[Climate risk splits four ways, not two]] | Physical risks divided into acute (floods, heat waves) and chronic (rising temperatures, sea-level rise), plus transition risks *and* opportunities as the second pillar. The classification is lifted from the CSRD directive rather than invented. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Beyond the Concrete – Designing Buildings That Last]] | — |
| [[Closed-loop water killed by human behaviour]] | A closed greywater and blackwater recycling concept developed with a Swedish partner was dropped because occupants flush cleaning chemicals, T-shirts and worse down warehouse toilets — an unacceptable risk for investor and tenant. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[From Model to Reality – Sustainability in Industrial Buildings]] | [[Water reuse share]] |
| [[Cloud in the city, not in the middle of nowhere]] | The framing of the whole lecture: data centres should be brought back into cities and treated as part of everyday urban life, rather than exiled to cheap land. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Cloud in the city – data centres, urban development and implications]] | [[Urban integration of logistics buildings]] |
| [[CLT was not the goal, it was the outcome]] | Penny never asked for a wooden building; it asked for something cheap, fast and repeatable across a 400-store estate. CLT won because YUAR pushed it as the most viable answer. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Build Your Own Playground – Architecture where no one looks for it]] | [[Embodied carbon and adaptive reuse of logistics buildings]] |
| [[Compliance passed by deleting a metre of floor]] | On the case study, the Czech daylight code was satisfied with almost no design change — a windowsill — plus a redefinition of the assessed zone, excluding the one-metre strip along the building core as circulation rather than administrative work. Described as standard practice. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Criteria for a Green Building – Beyond energy performance LEED - BREEAM - WELL]] | [[Worker comfort and well-being]] |
| [[Concentration of functions equals freedom elsewhere]] | Concentrating the visitor programme onto one part of the site is what frees the remaining space for vegetation and public function. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Challenge the perception of industrial architecture as 'boxing']] | [[Public access and community use of logistics sites]], [[Publicly accessible area]] |
| [[Concrete cannot stop and go]] | Plastic printers retract, travel and resume; concrete cannot, because pressure keeps building in the hose after the pump stops and must rebuild before flow restarts. Discontinuous geometries must be printed in whole sections, and the rough finish is a direct result of the technology. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Printing Against Gravity – From the construction site to the research lab]] | — |
| [[Concrete is a low-carbon material]] | Per kilogram, of five compared, steel is highest at ~1.55 kgCO2/kg (range 0.5–3), then mineral wool, then CLT, then plasterboard — and concrete is the *lowest*. The damage comes from volume, not intensity. He calls a mineral wool factory "like a mini volcano." | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Net Zero That Holds Up – Whole-Life Carbon, Design Choices, and Delivery]] | [[Embodied carbon and adaptive reuse of logistics buildings]], [[Embodied carbon intensity]] |
| [[Consolidation centres cut trips 20–50%]] | Urban consolidation centres, transferring goods from large vans into smaller cleaner vehicles, are proven to reduce freight vehicle trips by 20–50% in some cases — but effectiveness depends entirely on location and actual usage. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Integrated Urban Logistics – From last-mile to mobility hubs]] | [[Last-mile delivery emissions and traffic]], [[Thomas Olsen]], [[Eva Kažukolovaitė]] |
| [[Construction logistics is planning's blind spot]] | It makes up 20–30% of urban freight traffic, involves the heaviest vehicles with disproportionate impacts on congestion, emissions and road wear, is set to grow with urbanisation and renovation, and is nonetheless poorly coordinated and rarely integrated into planning. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Integrated Urban Logistics – From last-mile to mobility hubs]] | [[Road freight impact]] |
| [[Construction traffic dwarfs parcel traffic]] | Last-mile delivery accounts for roughly 3% of urban vehicle kilometres driven, while construction site logistics accounts for about 37% — yet EU policy and funding attention concentrates on the former. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-disputed">Disputed</span> | [[Integrated Urban Logistics – From last-mile to mobility hubs]] | [[Last-mile delivery emissions and traffic]] |
| [[Consumer impatience is a physical planning force]] | Because consumers expect near-instant delivery, urgency pushes warehousing closer to demand; goods that can wait one to three days by truck can sit in CEE and still serve Germany, France, Spain and Italy. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Why CEE is the Hottest Location in Europe for supply chain, logistics and industrial Development]] | [[Delivery time]] |
| [[Contrast, not imitation, next to a heritage building]] | Where a preserved solid masonry building meets a new industrial volume, they deliberately based the design on contrast — the new building fully translucent and light despite its size. And the harder case is named: a 30-year-old neighbour has no heritage value to anchor against. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Challenge the perception of industrial architecture as 'boxing']] | [[Visual impact and acceptance by neighbours]] |
| [[Converting a concrete frame to timber late is a disaster]] | Projects that take a concrete-frame design and try to make it timber at the end produce a timber building everyone hates because it was never designed as one — which then discredits timber. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Net Zero That Holds Up – Whole-Life Carbon, Design Choices, and Delivery]] | [[Embodied carbon and adaptive reuse of logistics buildings]] |
| [[Copenhagen's bike lanes took decades and cannot be retrofitted cheaply]] | Copenhagen's wide, kerb-raised bike lanes are an enabling system built over decades; real accommodation of new last-mile vehicles means widening lanes, and that is not a low-cost retrofit. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Integrated Urban Logistics – From last-mile to mobility hubs]] | [[Last-mile delivery emissions and traffic]] |
| [[Copenhagen's liveability worth EUR 5bn GDP]] | A Ramboll study claims Copenhagen's resilience boosted the city's GDP by 5 billion euro compared with 20 non-resilient European cities. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-disputed">Disputed</span> | [[From Plot to Place – Designing Thriving and Resilient Neighborhoods]] | — |
| [[CSRD is what actually buys the analysis]] | Companies most commonly commission climate risk assessment because they need compliance — CSRD non-financial reporting — or because a bank/investor requires it before financing. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Beyond the Concrete – Designing Buildings That Last]] | — |
| [[Cycling credits for a hall in a field]] | Cycling infrastructure carries substantial points, but a warehouse in the middle of nowhere has no cyclists; the building effectively loses points for a category meaningless in its context. Such criteria should be removable rather than scored. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Logistics Development in the concept of certification and LCA]] | — |
| [[Czech benchmarks built from ~117 buildings]] | UCEEB assembled roughly 117 Czech building LCA case studies and derived preliminary national benchmarks — the quoted 41 figure being the 66th percentile of all values. Explicitly described as a starting point, not a large statistical sample, yet this is how limits and green public procurement thresholds get made. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Logistics Development in the concept of certification and LCA]] | [[Whole-life carbon]] |
| [[Czech brownfields are the wrong brownfields]] | Unlike Germany, France or Spain, CZ has no stock of 1980s–90s light industrial sheds. Its brownfields are steel mills and heavy factories: hard to convert, too contaminated to clean economically for residential — so most are demolished. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Industrial Architecture as Living Infrastructure – Why future design is defined by systems, data, and adaptability, not just form]] | [[Embodied carbon and adaptive reuse of logistics buildings]] |
| [[Czech fire rules are stricter than the EU's — and these buildings are changing…]] | Fire regulation in Czechia is described as harder than the EU baseline, with a threshold effect above roughly 850 m² per room; they claim their timber stores are themselves pushing the regulation to change. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Build Your Own Playground – Architecture where no one looks for it]] | [[Embodied carbon and adaptive reuse of logistics buildings]] |
| [[Czech grid is the real siting constraint]] | A studio participant notes Czech data centres cluster near cities because of grid limitations, and that the de-urbanisation forecast only becomes real once energy and high-speed connectivity are solved. Parker agrees and adds that Central and Eastern Europe are currently in a weaker position on renewables transition than the Nordics or Spain, and should prioritise it structurally so nearby logistics and manufacturing can benefit. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[The Geography of Growth – Cities, AI, and the future of property]] | — |
| [[Czech height limits are quietly repelling investment]] | Fully automated warehouses require high-bay buildings (20 m+, up to 30 m). Czech master plans and municipalities effectively don't approve them; German and British investors know this and don't even enquire. Poland has just permitted 30 m buildings. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Why CEE is the Hottest Location in Europe for supply chain, logistics and industrial Development]] | [[Automation and operational efficiency of logistics]], [[Visual impact and acceptance by neighbours]], [[Adam Večeřa]] |
| [[Czech investment volumes are an artefact of illiquidity]] | A reported +250% jump is explained away: the Czech market is dominated by four or five developers such as CTP and VGP who build and hold rather than sell, unlike Panattoni. Two portfolio trades produce a headline spike; this year could be "down 80%". | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Performing Despite the Headwinds – Understanding the drivers of European logistics performance]] | — |
| [[Czech legal emission factor is double reality]] | In one case study, switching to a Norwegian-style green electricity mix roughly halved the carbon footprint versus Czech figures — but she adds that the carbon emission factor written into Czech legislation is about twice the real value, so the modelled impacts are overstated. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Logistics Development in the concept of certification and LCA]] | [[Operational carbon]] |
| [[Czech mayors have almost no reason to say yes]] | For a 40,000 m² occupier with around 400 employees over a 30-year horizon, the municipality itself receives roughly 22 million CZK in Czechia, versus a far larger share in Germany — while national tax revenues are broadly comparable between the two countries. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[From Model to Reality – Sustainability in Industrial Buildings]] | [[Social acceptance]] |
| [[Czech Republic went first; Germany followed]] | The CLT store was built in Czechia before the German parent group took the same decision one to two years later. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Build Your Own Playground – Architecture where no one looks for it]] | — |
| [[Danish pit-storage heat pumps match warehouse scale]] | Danish district systems combine large heat-pump units with water-pit thermal storage serving a village or city district; their megawatt capacity is of the same order as a logistics building's, which is why he reads them as the model for the sector's future. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[From Model to Reality – Sustainability in Industrial Buildings]] | [[Operational energy and on-site renewables]] |
| [[Data centre carbon is many multiples of a warehouse]] | Ramboll's late-2025 white paper charts a typical logistics warehouse against a data centre; the difference, both operational and upfront, is "many multiples." Normal building rules of thumb do not transfer. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Net Zero That Holds Up – Whole-Life Carbon, Design Choices, and Delivery]] | [[Operational carbon]] |
| [[Data centre sizes are commercially opaque]] | Asked for the minimum viable size or typology of an AI-capable data centre, he admits the data is not available: actual facility sizes and changing capacities are confidential information forecasters cannot access. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[The Geography of Growth – Cities, AI, and the future of property]] | — |
| [[Data centres are giant heaters]] | They must hold ~100% uptime through the hottest summer day, so they need chiller capacity sized for the worst case regardless of waste-heat recovery. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Net Zero That Holds Up – Whole-Life Carbon, Design Choices, and Delivery]] | [[Waste heat and cooling of data centres]], [[Waste heat recovery]], [[Julia Godard Lombard]] |
| [[Data centres now outbid logistics for land, power and capital]] | A direct competitor on three fronts simultaneously, expected to intensify. Notably they do *not* compete for labour. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Performing Despite the Headwinds – Understanding the drivers of European logistics performance]] | — |
| [[Data centres outgrowing the FLAP cities]] | Europe's data centres clustered in Frankfurt, London, Amsterdam, Paris and Dublin because financial-sector low-latency needs put them there. As AI diffuses beyond finance, demand is dispersing to Madrid, Milan, Vienna, Warsaw and Nordic cities. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[The Geography of Growth – Cities, AI, and the future of property]] | — |
| [[Data centres will de-urbanise in 10–15 years]] | Beyond the current 3–4 year visible pipeline he expects a de-urbanisation trend: as cloud technology matures and latency constraints ease, data centres move to isolated rural areas where costs are lower. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[The Geography of Growth – Cities, AI, and the future of property]] | — |
| [[Data is the foundation of the whole pyramid]] | Without LCA data you cannot calculate indicators, set limits, attach incentives, or legislate — so databases sit beneath LCA, politics, legislation and finally sustainability. The relevant standards are EN 15978 (buildings) and EN 15804+A2 (products), developed over 15 years. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Logistics Development in the concept of certification and LCA]] | — |
| [[Daylight buys 18% productivity, retail 15–40%]] | Same survey as cited: real daylight made workers 18% more productive; retail sales rose 15–40% where people felt better; better lighting plus individual temperature control raised productivity by 23%. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-disputed">Disputed</span> | [[Criteria for a Green Building – Beyond energy performance LEED - BREEAM - WELL]] | [[Worker comfort and well-being]], [[Worker well-being]] |
| [[Daylighting a river that was a technical problem]] | A Singapore project from ~20 years ago reversed a tunnelled watercourse under a park; what was once solved technically now handles storm volumes classical infrastructure cannot, and became a public place people play in. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Nature positive by design – Creating lasting impact by designing for and with nature]] | [[Stormwater and sealed surfaces on logistics sites]] |
| [[Defence spending exposed roads that can't carry tanks]] | When defence emerged as an economic driver, it was pointed out that many European roads and bridges cannot cope with tanks. | [[Delivery robots have no legal place in the street section]] | Autonomous delivery robots raise an unresolved space-allocation question — sidewalk, roadway, or dedicated robot lanes, which do not exist anywhere in Europe — and the question of who benefits must be weighed before deployment. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Integrated Urban Logistics – From last-mile to mobility hubs]] | [[Last-mile delivery emissions and traffic]] |
| [[Density is the environmental strategy]] | Maximise utilisation of the plot so you do not consume more free space outside the city. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Industrial Architecture as Living Infrastructure – Why future design is defined by systems, data, and adaptability, not just form]] | [[Land take of logistics buildings]], [[Land take]] |
| [[Design challenges live at the city's edges]] | Designers have concentrated on urban squares and city centres while the genuinely exciting and unaddressed design problems sit at the periphery, where industry and logistics land. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Nature positive by design – Creating lasting impact by designing for and with nature]] | — |
| [[Design for evolution, not for completion]] | Buildings, landscapes and infrastructure should be designed with the ability to transform, adapt and regenerate over time, on the model of living systems. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Nature positive by design – Creating lasting impact by designing for and with nature]] | — |
| [[Design is the only lever architects hold]] | Printability depends on material, printing parameters, hardware and environmental conditions — none of which architects control — plus design, split into geometry and slicing. Design has the highest impact on printability and is among the easiest factors to modify. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[Printing Against Gravity – From the construction site to the research lab]] | — |
| [[Design's object is now invisible]] | Briefs used to be plainly visible; today the design material is hidden knowledge — safety, microclimate, nature. | <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--source rl-badge--source-expert">Expert</span> <span class="rl-badge rl-badge--evidence rl-badge--evidence-open-for-review">Open for expert review</span> | [[From Plot to Place – Designing Thriving and Resilient Neighborhoods]] | — |
| [[Designed in a year, permitted for years]] | Their most troublesome project has a facade of perforated steel mesh carrying the tread profile of a Continental tyre, with six towers hidden behind the walls.
